## II A Night’s Ride / II 夜驰

===Original===

Harry jumped from the ground, kissed his wife, called her “old girl,” and told her to be happy, and got on his horse at the garden gate. Both the ladies came off the veranda to see him start.

“It’s as dark as pitch,” said Kate Daly.

“That’s because you have just come out of the light.”

“But it is dark﻿—quite dark. You won’t be late, will you?” said the wife.

“I can’t be very early, as it’s near ten now. I shall be back about twelve.” So saying, he broke at once into a gallop, and vanished into the night, his young groom scampering after him.

===Chinese===

哈里从地上一跃而起，吻了吻妻子，唤她一声“老婆子”，叫她只管开心些，随即在花园门口上了马。两位女士都从游廊上走出来，为他送行。

“黑得像沥青一样。”凯特·戴利（Kate Daly）说。

“那是因为你刚从亮处出来。”

“可天就是黑——黑得很。你不会回来得太晚吧？”妻子说。

“现在都快十点了，我没法太早回来。大约十二点我就到家了。”说着，他立刻纵马飞奔起来，消失在夜色之中，他的小马童在后面一路小跑地跟着。

===Original===

“Why should he go out now?” Kate said to her sister.

“He is afraid of fire.”

“But he can’t prevent the fires by riding about in the dark. I suppose the fires come from the heat.”

“He thinks they come from enemies, and he has heard something. One wretched man may do so much when everything is dried to tinder. I do so wish it would rain.”

The night, in truth, was very dark. It was now midsummer, at which time with us the days are so long that the coming of the one almost catches the departure of its predecessor. But Gangoil was not far outside the tropics, and there were no long summer nights. The heat was intense; but there was a low soughing wind which seemed to moan among the trees without moving them. As they crossed the little home enclosure and the horse paddock, the track was just visible, the trees being dead and the spaces open. About half a mile from the house, while they were still in the horse paddock, Harry turned from the track, and Jacko, of course, turned with him.

===Chinese===

“他为什么这会儿还要出去？”凯特问姐姐。

“他怕失火。”

“可他摸黑骑马到处转，也防不了火呀。我看这些火灾是天太热引起的。”

“他觉得火是仇人放的，他还听到了些风声。如今样样东西都干得像火绒，一个恶棍就能闯下大祸。我多盼着下场雨啊。”

夜色着实黑得很。此时正值仲夏。在我们那里，这个时节白昼长得很，新的一天到来时，前一天几乎还没走。可冈戈伊尔（Gangoil）离热带不远，没有漫长的夏夜。天气酷热难当；只有一阵低低的风声呜咽着，仿佛在树间悲鸣，却撼不动一片枝叶。他们穿过宅旁的小围地和马圈时，小径尚可辨认——树木都已枯死，空地开阔。离宅子约半英里、还在马圈里的时候，哈里便离开小径拐了出去，贾科（Jacko）当然也跟着拐。

===Original===

“You can sit your horse jumping, Jacko?” he asked.

“My word! jump like glory,” answered Jacko.

He was soon tried. Harry rode at the bush fence﻿—which was not, indeed, much of a fence, made of logs lengthways and crossways, about three feet and a half high﻿—and went over it. Jacko followed him, rushing his horse at the leap, losing his seat and almost falling over the animal’s shoulders as he came to the ground.

“My word!” said Jacko, just saving himself by a scramble; “who ever saw the like of that?”

“Why don’t you sit in your saddle, you stupid young duffer?”

===Chinese===

“贾科，你骑马跳障碍的时候坐得住吧？”他问。

“好家伙！跳得可漂亮啦。”贾科答道。

很快就考验到了他。哈里策马冲向那道灌木篱笆——其实那也算不上什么篱笆，不过是纵横架起的圆木，约三英尺半高——一跃而过。贾科跟着冲去跳时，在马背上坐不稳，落地时险些从马脖子上栽下去。

“好家伙！”贾科手忙脚乱才算稳住身子，说道，“谁见过这样的跳法？”

“你怎么不坐稳鞍子，你这个蠢笨的小傻瓜？”

===Original===

“Sit in my saddle! Why don’t he jump proper? Well, you go on. I don’t know that I’m a duffer. Duffer, indeed! My word!”

Heathcote had turned to the left, leaving the track, which was, indeed, the main road toward the nearest town and the coast, and was now pushing on through the forest with no pathway at all to guide him. To ordinary eyes the attempt to steer any course would have been hopeless. But an Australian squatter, if he have any well-grounded claim to the character of a bushman, has eyes which are not ordinary, and he has, probably, nurtured within himself, unconsciously, topographical instincts which are unintelligible to the inhabitants of cities. Harry, too, was near his own home, and went forward through the thick gloom without a doubt, Jacko following him faithfully. In about half an hour they came to another fence, but now it was too absolutely dark for jumping. Harry had not seen it till he was close to it, and then he pulled up his horse.

“My word! why don’t you jump away, Mr. Harry? Who’s a duffer now?”

“Hold your tongue, or I’ll put my whip across your back. Get down and help me pull a log away. The horses couldn’t see where to put their feet.”

Jacko did as he was bid, and worked hard, but still grumbled at having been called a duffer. The animals were quickly led over, the logs were replaced, and the two were again galloping through the forest. “I thought you were making for the wool-shed,” said Jacko.

===Chinese===

“坐稳鞍子！他自己咋不跳得像样点？行啦，您先走吧。我不知道我哪点傻。还傻瓜呢！好家伙！”

希思科特向左拐去，离开了那条小径——那其实是通向最近市镇和海岸的大路——此刻正穿行在森林之中，全无路径可循。在寻常人眼里，想认准任何方向都是绝无指望的。但一个澳大利亚牧场主，只要他当真有资格称得上丛林人，就有一双不同寻常的眼睛，而且多半还在不知不觉间养成了一种地形本能，那是城里居民无法理解的。哈里又是在自家附近，于是在浓重的黑暗中毫不迟疑地前行，贾科忠实地跟着。约莫半个钟头后，他们又遇到一道篱笆，但这回天色实在太黑，跳不得了。哈里到了近前才看见它，赶紧勒住了马。

“好家伙！您咋不跳了，哈里先生？这会儿谁傻呀？”

“住口，不然我拿鞭子抽你的背。下马来，帮我把一根木头拉开。马看不见往哪儿下蹄子。”

贾科依言下了马，干得很卖力，嘴里却还嘟囔着不服那个“傻瓜”的称呼。两匹马很快被牵了过去，圆木重新放好，两人又策马飞奔在森林里。“我还当您是奔剪毛棚去的呢。”贾科说。

===Original===

“We’re eight miles beyond the wool-shed,” said Harry. They had now crossed another paddock, and had come to the extreme fence on the run. The Gangoil pastures extended much further, but in that direction had not as yet been enclosed. Here they both got off their horses and walked along the fence till they came to an opening, with a slip panel, or movable bars, which had been Heathcote’s intended destination. “Hold the horses, Jacko, till I come back,” he said.

Jacko, when alone, nothing daunted by the darkness or solitude, seated himself on the top rail, took out a pipe, and struck a match. When the tobacco was ignited he dropped the match on the dry grass at his feet, and a little flame instantly sprang up. The boy waited a few seconds till the flames began to run, and then putting his feet together on the ground stamped out the incipient fire. “My word!” said Jacko to himself, “it’s easy done, anyway.”

Harry went on to the left for about half a mile, and then stood leaning against the fence. It was very dark, but he was now looking over into an enclosure which had been altogether cleared of trees, and which, as he knew well, had been cultivated and was covered with sugarcanes. Where he stood he was not distant above a quarter of a mile from the river, and the field before him ran down to the banks. This was the selected land of Giles Medlicot﻿—two years since a portion of his own run, which had now been purchased from the government﻿—for the loss of which he had received and was entitled to receive no compensation. And the matter was made worse for him by the fact that the interloper had come between him and the river. But he was not standing here near midnight merely to exercise his wrath by straining his eyes through the darkness at his neighbour’s crops. He put his finger into his mouth to wet it, and then held it up that he might discover which way the light breath of wind was coming. There was still the low moan to be heard continually through the forest, and yet not a leaf seemed to be moved.

After a while he thought he caught a sound, and put his ear down to the ground. He distinctly heard a footstep, and rising up, walked quickly toward the spot whence the noise came.

“Who’s that?” he said, as he saw the figure of a man standing on his side of the fence, and leaning against it, with a pipe in his month.

===Chinese===

“咱们这儿离剪毛棚还有八英里呢。”哈里说。这时他们已穿过另一片围场，来到牧场最外围的篱笆跟前。冈戈伊尔的草场往外延伸得远得多，只是那个方向至今还没有圈起来。两人在这里下了马，沿着篱笆走到一处开口——那儿装着活动栏门，也就是可以挪动的横栏——这正是希思科特此行的目的地。“牵住马，贾科，等我回来。”他说。

贾科独自留下时，丝毫没被黑暗和孤寂吓住，跨坐在篱笆顶栏上，掏出烟斗，划了一根火柴。烟点着之后，他把火柴丢在脚边的干草上，一小簇火苗立刻蹿了起来。这孩子等了几秒钟，直到火苗开始蔓延，才把双脚并拢踩到地上，把初起的火踩灭。“好家伙！”贾科自言自语道，“反正这事儿容易得很。”

哈里向左又走了约半英里，然后站住，倚在篱笆上。天很黑，但他此刻眺望的是一片树木已全部清光的围地，他清楚那地已被人耕种，长满了甘蔗。他站的地方离河不到四分之一英里，眼前的田地一直延伸到河岸。这是吉尔斯·梅德利科特（Giles Medlicot）选买的土地——两年前还是他自己牧场的一部分，如今已由对方从政府手里买去——他为此蒙受损失，却既未得到、也无权得到分文补偿。更让他恼火的是，这个闯入者偏偏楔在了他和河流之间。但他半夜三更站在这儿，倒不只是为了在黑暗中瞪着邻居的庄稼宣泄怒气。他把手指伸进嘴里蘸湿，然后举起来，好弄清那一丝微风从哪个方向吹来。森林里依旧不断响着低低的呜咽声，然而却没有一片叶子像是在动。

过了一会儿，他觉得捕捉到了一点声响，便把耳朵贴到地上。他清楚地听见一阵脚步声，于是站起身，快步朝声音传来的地方走去。

“是谁？”他问道，这时他看见篱笆自己这一侧站着一个人影，正倚在篱笆上，嘴里叼着烟斗。

===Original===

“Who are you?” replied the man on the fence. “My name is Medlicot.”

“Oh, Mr. Medlicot, is it?”

“Is that Mr. Heathcote? Good night, Mr. Heathcote. You are going about at a late hour of the night.”

“I have to go about early and late; but I ain’t later than you.”

“I’m close at home,” said Medlicot.

===Chinese===

“你是谁？”篱笆旁那人答道，“我叫梅德利科特。”

“哦，是梅德利科特先生？”

“是希思科特先生吗？晚上好，希思科特先生。夜里这么晚您还四处走动。”

“我早晚都得四处巡查；不过我可不比你晚。”

“我就在自家门口。”梅德利科特说。

===Original===

“I am, at any rate, on my own run,” said Harry.

“You mean to say that I am trespassing?” said the other; “because I can very soon jump back over the fence.”

“I didn’t mean that at all, Mr. Medlicot; anybody is welcome on my run, night or day, who knows how to behave himself.”

“I hope I’m included in that list.”

“Just so﻿—of course. Considering the state that everything is in, and all the damage that a fire would do, I rather wish that people would be a little more careful about smoking.”

===Chinese===

“我至少还在自家的牧场上。”哈里说。

“你是说我擅闯了？”对方说，“要这么说，我很快就能跳回篱笆那边去。”

“我根本不是那个意思，梅德利科特先生；无论白天黑夜，只要懂得自重，谁都欢迎到我的牧场上来。”

“希望我也在这份名单里。”

“正是——当然。考虑到眼下万物干枯的情形，还有一场大火会造成的种种损失，我倒是希望人们抽烟时多几分小心。”

===Original===

“My canes, Mr. Heathcote, would burn quite as quickly as your grass.”

“It is not only the grass. I’ve a hundred miles of fencing on the run which is as dry as tinder, not to talk of the station and the wool-shed.”

“They shan’t suffer from my neglect, Mr. Heathcote.”

“You have men about who mayn’t be so careful. The wind, such as it is, is coming right across from your place. If there were light enough, I could show you three or four patches where there has been fire within half a mile of this spot. There was a log burning there for two or three days, not long ago, which was lighted by one of your men.”

“That was a fortnight since. There was no heat then, and the men were boiling their kettle. I spoke about it.”

===Chinese===

“我的甘蔗，希思科特先生，烧起来跟你的草一样快。”

“不光是草。我牧场上有一百英里的篱笆，干得像火绒一样，更别提场部和剪毛棚了。”

“它们绝不会因为我的疏忽遭殃，希思科特先生。”

“可你手下的工人未必都这么细心。眼下这点风，正是从你那边径直吹过来的。要是光线够亮，我能指给你看三四块过火的痕迹，就在离这儿半英里之内。不久前有一根木头在那儿烧了两三天，就是你的一个工人点着的。”

“那是两个星期前的事了。那会儿天还不热，工人们在烧水沏茶。我说过他们。”

===Original===

“A log like that, Mr. Medlicot, will burn for weeks sometimes. I’ll tell you fairly what I’m afraid of. There’s a man with you whom I turned out of the shed last shearing, and I think he might put a match down﻿—not by accident.”

“You mean Nokes. As far as I know, he’s a decent man. You wouldn’t have me not employ a man just because you had dismissed him?”

“Certainly not﻿—that is, I shouldn’t think of dictating to you about such a thing.”

“Well, no, Mr. Heathcote, I suppose not. Nokes has got to earn his bread, though you did dismiss him. I don’t know that he’s not as honest a man as you or I.”

“If so, there’s three of us very bad﻿—that’s all, Mr. Medlicot. Good night﻿—and if you’ll trouble yourself to look after the ash of your tobacco it might be the saving of me and all I have.” So saying, he turned round, and made his way back to the horses.

===Chinese===

“那样一根木头，梅德利科特先生，有时能烧上好几个星期。我坦白告诉你我怕的是什么。你那儿有个人，上次剪羊毛时被我撵出了棚子，我看他完全可能划火柴放火——可不是无心之失。”

“你说的是诺克斯（Nokes）。据我所知，他是个正派人。总不能就因为你辞退了他，我就不能雇人了吧？”

“当然不是——就是说，这种事我绝没有想对你发号施令的意思。”

“嗯，是啊，希思科特先生，我想你也不会。诺克斯总得挣口饭吃，虽说你的确辞退了他。我不觉得他就不如你我诚实。”

“要真那样，咱们仨就都够坏的——就这么回事，梅德利科特先生。晚安——劳您驾看管好您的烟灰，兴许就能救下我和我的全部家当。”说着，他转过身，朝马匹走回去。

===Original===

Medlicot had placed himself on the fence during the interview, and he still kept his seat. Of course he was now thinking of the man who had just left him, whom he declared to himself to be an ignorant, prejudiced, ill-constituted cur. “I believe in his heart he thinks that I’m going to set fire to his run,” he said, almost aloud. “And because he grows wool he thinks himself above everybody in the colony. He occupies thousands of acres, and employs three or four men. I till about two hundred, and maintain thirty families. But he is such a pig that he can’t understand all that; and he thinks that I must be something low because I’ve bought with my own money a bit of land which never belonged to him, and which he couldn’t use.” Such was the nature of Giles Medlicot’s soliloquy as he sat swinging his legs, and still smoking his pipe, on the fence which divided his sugarcane from the other young man’s run.

And Harry Heathcote uttered his soliloquy also. “I wouldn’t swear that he wouldn’t do it himself, after all;” meaning that he almost suspected that Medlicot himself would be an incendiary. To him, in his way of thinking, a man who would take advantage of the law to buy a bit of another man’s land﻿—or become a free-selector, as the term goes﻿—was a public enemy, and might be presumed capable of any iniquity. It was all very well for the girls﻿—meaning his wife and sister-in-law﻿—to tell him that Medlicot had the manners of a gentleman and had come of decent people. Women were always soft enough to be taken by soft hands, a good-looking face, and a decent coat. This Medlicot went about dressed like a man in the towns, exhibiting, as Harry thought, a contemptible, unmanly finery. Of what use was it to tell him that Medlicot was a gentleman? What Harry knew was that since Medlicot had come he had lost his sheep, that the heads of three or four had been found buried on Medlicot’s side of his run, and that if he dismissed “a hand,” Medlicot employed him﻿—a proceeding which, in Harry Heathcote’s aristocratic and patriarchal views of life, was altogether ungentleman-like. How were the “hands” to be kept in their place if one employer of labor did not back up another?

He had been warned to be on his guard against fire. The warnings had hardly been implicit, but yet had come in a shape which made him unable to ignore them. Old Bates, whom he trusted implicitly, and who was a man of very few words, had told him to be on his guard. The German, at whose hut he had been in the morning, Karl Bender by name, and a servant of his own, had told him that there would be fire about before long. “Why should anyone want to ruin me?” Harry had asked. “Did I ever wrong a man of a shilling?” The German had learned to know his young master, had made his way through the crust of his master’s character, and was prepared to be faithful at all points﻿—though he too could have quarrelled and have avenged himself had it not chanced that he had come to the point of loving instead of hating his employer.

“You like too much to be governor over all,” said the German, as he stooped over the fire in his own hut in his anxiety to boil the water for Heathcote’s tea.

“Somebody must be governor, or everything would go to the devil!” said Harry.

===Chinese===

交谈期间，梅德利科特一直跨坐在篱笆上，此刻仍稳稳坐着。他心里自然在想刚离开他的那个人，暗自断定那是个无知、偏执、天生心术不正的卑劣之徒。“我看他心里八成觉得我要放火烧他的牧场。”他几乎是出声地说。“就因为他养羊，他便觉得自己高踞全殖民地所有人之上。他占着几千英亩地，雇着三四个工人。我耕种着两百英亩上下，养活着三十户人家。可他蠢得像头猪，根本不明白这些；还因为我用自己的钱买了一小块从不属于他、他也用不上的地，就觉得我必定出身低贱。”吉尔斯·梅德利科特就这样坐在篱笆上晃着两条腿，抽着烟斗，自言自语地发着议论——这道篱笆，隔开了他的甘蔗地和那个年轻人的牧场。

哈里·希思科特也道出了他的独白。“我没法发誓说他本人就一定干不出来；”意思是说，他几乎疑心梅德利科特本人就是个纵火犯。在他的观念里，一个会钻法律的空子买下别人牧场一角的人——按通行的说法，做个自由选地农（free-selector）——就是公众之敌，就可以被推定干得出任何伤天害理的事。姑娘们——指他的妻子和小姨子——对他说梅德利科特有绅士派头、出身体面人家，那又顶什么用。女人家的心肠总是软，一双温柔的手、一张漂亮的脸蛋、一件体面的外衣，就足以把她们迷住。这位梅德利科特到处走动，穿得像城里人一般讲究，在哈里看来，透着一股可鄙的、没有男子气的浮华。跟他说梅德利科特是绅士又有什么用？哈里知道的是：自打梅德利科特来了，他就丢了羊，有三四只羊的头骨被发现埋在他牧场边界靠梅德利科特那一侧；他若辞掉一个“工人”，梅德利科特便雇下那人——在哈里·希思科特那套贵族式、家长式的人生观念里，这等行径全然不像绅士所为。要是雇工的东家之间不互相撑腰，“工人”们还怎么各安其分？

先前有人警告过他，要提防火灾。这些警告虽说得并不直白，但其来路又叫他无法置之不理。老贝茨（Bates）是他绝对信得过的人，又是个极少开口的人，也告诉过他要提防。那个德国人——早上他刚去过那人的小屋，那人名叫卡尔·本德（Karl Bender），是他自家的雇工——也告诉过他，不久就会起火。“为什么有人想毁了我？”哈里曾问，“我可曾亏过谁一个先令？”这德国人已经摸透了年轻主人的脾性，穿透了他性格的外壳，准备在一切事情上对他忠心——虽说若不是碰巧对这位雇主生出了爱而非恨，他本来也可能会吵翻，可能会报复。

“您太喜欢当众人的总督啦。”德国人一面俯在自己小屋的炉火前，急着替希思科特烧水沏茶，一面说道。

“总得有人当总督，不然一切都得见鬼去！”哈里说。

===Original===

“Dat’s true﻿—only fellows don’t like be made feel it,” said the German, “Nokes, he was made feel it when you put him over de gate.”

But neither would Bates nor the German express absolute suspicion of any man. That Medlicot’s “hands” at the sugar-mill were stealing his sheep Harry thought that he knew; but that was comparatively a small affair, and he would not have pressed it, as he was without absolute evidence. And even he had a feeling that it would be unwise to increase the anger felt against himself﻿—at any rate, during the present heats.

Jacko had his pipe still alight when Heathcote returned. “You young monkey,” said he, “have you been using matches?”

“Why not, Mr. Harry? Don’t the grass burn ready, Mr. Harry? My word!” Then Jacko stooped down, lit another match, and showed Heathcote the burned patch.

“Was it so when we came?” Harry asked, with emotion. Jacko, still kneeling on the ground, and holding the lighted match in his hand, shook his head and tapped his breast, indicating that he had burned the grass. “You dropped the match by accident?”

===Chinese===

“这话不假——只是伙计们不喜欢让人家点破这一点。”德国人说，“诺克斯，您把他从大门上扔出去那回，他就被点破过啦。”

但无论贝茨还是那德国人，都不肯把对任何人的明确怀疑说出口。糖厂那边梅德利科特的“工人”们在偷他的羊，哈里觉得这一点自己心中有数；但那终归是件小事，况且他没有确凿的证据，也就不愿深究。就连他也感觉到，再给人添怨火上浇油，未免不明智——至少眼下这段酷热的日子里不能。

希思科特回来时，贾科的烟斗还燃着。“你这只小猴崽子，”他说，“你划火柴来着？”

“为啥不划呢，哈里先生？这草不是一点就着嘛，哈里先生？好家伙！”说着贾科弯下腰，又划着一根火柴，把烧过的那块地指给希思科特看。

“我们来的时候就是这样吗？”哈里激动地问。贾科仍跪在地上，手里举着点着的火柴，摇了摇头，又拍拍自己的胸口，表示草是他烧的。“你是失手掉了火柴？”

===Original===

“My word! no. Did it o’ purpose to see. It’s all just one as gunpowder, Mr. Harry.”

Harry got on his horse without a word, and rode away through the forest, taking a direction different from that by which he had come, and the boy followed him. He was by no means certain that this young fellow might not turn against him; but it had been a part of his theory to make no difference to any man because of such fears. If he could make the men around him respect him, then they would treat him well; but they could never be brought to respect him by flattery. He was very nearly right in his views of men, and would have been right altogether could he have seen accurately what justice demanded for others as well as for himself. As far as the intention went, he was minded to be just to every man.

It seemed, as they were riding, that the heat grew fiercer and fiercer. Though there was still the same moaning sound, there was not a breath of air. They had now got upon a track very well known to Heathcote, which led up from the river to the wool-shed, and so on to the station, and they had turned homeward. When they were near the wool-shed, suddenly there fell a heavy drop or two of rain. Harry stopped and turned his face upward, when, in a moment, the whole heavens above them and the forest around were illumined by a flash of lightning so near them that it made each of them start in his saddle, and made the horses shudder in every limb. Then came the roll of thunder immediately over their heads, and with the thunder rain so thick and fast that Harry’s “ten thousand buckets” seemed to be emptied directly over their heads.

“God A’mighty has put out the fires now,” said Jacko.

Harry paused for a moment, feeling the rain through to his bones, for he had nothing on over his shirt, and rejoicing in it. “Yes,” he said; “we may go to bed for a week, and let the grass grow, and the creeks fill, and the earth cool. Half an hour like this over the whole run, and there won’t be a dry stick on it.” As they went on, the horses splashed through the water. It seemed as though a deluge were falling, and that already the ground beneath their feet were becoming a lake.

“We might have too much of this, Jacko.”

===Chinese===

“好家伙！不是。是故意点着瞧瞧的。这草简直跟火药一个样，哈里先生。”

哈里一言不发地上了马，穿过森林骑马离去，走的方向与来时不同，那孩子跟在后面。他并非不疑心这个小家伙日后倒戈相向；但他的处世信条之一，就是绝不因这类疑惧而对任何人另眼相待。他若能让身边的人敬重他，他们便会待他好；可靠奉承是永远换不来敬重的。他看人的眼光大体不差，倘若他还能看清正义对别人的要求一如对自己的要求，那就全然无差了。就心意而论，他是有心要善待每一个人的。

一路骑去，暑气似乎愈发逼人。呜咽声依旧，却没有一丝风的气息。这时他们走上了一条希思科特十分熟悉的小径，从河边直通剪毛棚，再通往场部，他们已经踏上归途了。快到剪毛棚时，忽然沉甸甸地落下一两滴雨。哈里勒住马，仰起脸来，刹那间，他们头顶的整片天空和四周的森林被一道闪电照亮，闪电近得吓人，惊得两人都在鞍上猛地一颤，马匹也四肢发抖。接着雷声就在头顶上滚过，伴着雷声，雨点又急又密，哈里念叨的“一万只吊桶”仿佛就直接倒扣在他们头顶。

“全能的上帝这回把火都浇灭啦。”贾科说。

哈里驻马片刻，任雨水浸透骨髓——他衬衫外头什么也没穿——心里却为此欢喜。“是啊，”他说，“咱们尽可以睡上一个礼拜，让草长起来，让溪流灌满，让大地凉透。这样的雨在整个牧场上下上半个钟头，就再没有一根干柴了。”他们继续赶路，马匹哗啦哗啦地蹚水而行。仿佛天倾大河，脚下的地面眼看就要变成一片湖泽。

“这雨下过头了也受不了，贾科。”

===Original===

“My word, yes!”

“I don’t want to have the Mary flooded again.”

“My word, no!”

But by the time they reached the wool-shed it was over. From the first drop to the last, there had hardly been a space of twenty minutes. But there was a noise of waters as the little streams washed hither and thither to their destined courses and still the horses splashed, and still there was the feeling of an incipient deluge. When they reached the wool-shed, Harry again got off his horse, and Jacko, dismounting also, hitched the two animals to the post and followed his master into the building. Harry struck a wax match, and holding it up, strove to look round the building by the feeble light which it shed. It was a remarkable edifice, built in the shape of a great T, open at the sides, with a sharp-pitched timber roof covered with felt, which came down within four feet of the ground. It was calculated to hold about four hundred sheep at a time, and was divided into pens of various sizes, partitioned off for various purposes. If Harry Heathcote was sure of anything, he was sure that his wool-shed was the best that had ever been built in this district.

“By Jimini! what’s that?” said Jacko.

===Chinese===

“好家伙，可不是！”

“我可不想玛丽河再发大水。”

“好家伙，不想！”

可等他们到达剪毛棚，雨已经停了。从第一滴到最后一点，前后还不足二十分钟。但小溪四处奔流、各归水道，水声哗然不绝，马匹仍在蹚水，依然让人觉着一场大洪水正在发端。到了剪毛棚，哈里再次下马，贾科也跳下马来，把两匹牲口拴在柱子上，跟着主人进了棚。哈里划着一根蜡火柴，把它举起来，借那点微弱的光亮努力打量棚内。这是座引人注目的建筑，呈一个大大的“T”字形，四面敞开，架着高陡的木屋顶，上覆油毛毡，一直披到离地四英尺处。它按设计可同时容纳约四百只羊，分隔成大小不等的围栏，各有各的用途。如果哈里·希思科特对什么事有把握的话，那就是他的剪毛棚是本地区历来造得最好的一座。

“我的天哪！那是什么？”贾科说。

===Original===

“Did you hear anything?” Jacko pointed with his finger down the centre walk of the shed, and Harry, striking another match as he went, rushed forward. But the match was out as soon as ignited, and gave no glimmer of light. Nevertheless he saw, or thought that he saw, the figure of a man escaping out of the open end of the shed. The place itself was black as midnight, but the space beyond was clear of trees, and the darkness outside being a few shades lighter than within the building, allowed something of the outline of a figure to be visible. And as the man escaped, the sounds of his footsteps were audible enough. Harry called to him, but of course received no answer. Had he pursued him, he would have been obliged to cross sundry rails, which would have so delayed him as to give him no chance of success. “I knew there was a fellow about,” he said; “one of our own men would not have run like that.” Jacko shook his head, but did not speak. “He has got in here for shelter out of the rain, but he was doing no good about the place.” Jacko again shook his head. “I wonder who he was?”

Jacko came up and whispered in his ear, “Bill Nokes.”

“You couldn’t see him.”

“Seed the drag of his leg.” Now it was well known that the man Nokes had injured some of his muscles, and habitually dragged one foot after another.

“I don’t think you could have been sure of him by such a glimpse as that.”

===Chinese===

“你听见什么动静了吗？”贾科用手指朝棚子中央的通道指去，哈里边走边又划着一根火柴，猛冲上前。可火柴一点着就灭了，没透出一丝光亮。尽管如此，他还是看见了——或者自以为看见了——一条人影正从棚子敞开的那一头逃出去。棚内黑得如同子夜，但棚外那片空地没有树木，外头的黑暗比棚里浅了几分，人影的轮廓依稀可辨。那人逃走时，脚步声听得清清楚楚。哈里朝他喊了一声，当然没有得到回应。倘若追出去，他就得跨过好几道横杆，那样一耽搁，便绝无追上的可能。“我就知道有个人在这儿转悠，”他说，“咱们自己人不会那样逃跑。”贾科摇摇头，没有作声。“他钻进来是躲雨的，可他在这儿绝没干什么好事。”贾科又摇了摇头。“我纳闷他是谁呢？”

贾科凑上前来，贴着他耳朵悄声说：“比尔·诺克斯（Bill Nokes）。”

“你不可能看清他。”

“瞧见他拖腿的样子啦。”原来众所周知，诺克斯这人伤过几处筋肉，走路总是一条腿拖着另一条走。

“就那么一晃眼的工夫，我看你未必认得准。”

===Original===

“Maybe not,” said the boy, “only I’m sure as sure.”

Harry Heathcote said not another word, but getting again upon his horse, galloped home. It was past one when he reached the station, but the two girls were waiting up for him, and at once began to condole with him because he was wet.

“Wet!” said Harry; “if you could only know how much I prefer things being wet to dry just at present! But give Jacko some supper. I must keep that young fellow in good humour if I can.”

So Jacko had half a loaf of bread, and a small pot of jam, and a large jug of cold tea provided for him, in the enjoyment of which luxuries he did not seem to be in the least impeded by the fact that he was wet through to the skin. Harry Heathcote had another nobbler﻿—being only the second in the day﻿—and then went to bed.

===Chinese===

“兴许认不准，”那孩子说，“可我就是千真万确地认准了。”

哈里·希思科特再没说一句话，重新上马，飞驰回家。到场部时已过一点钟，姑娘俩还熬着夜等他，一见他浑身湿透，立刻连声心疼起来。

“湿透！”哈里说，“你们要是知道这会儿我多盼着东西是湿的而不是干的，就好了！不过得给贾科弄点晚饭。我得尽量让那小家伙保持好心情。”

于是贾科得到了半条面包、一小罐果酱和一大壶凉茶；他浑身湿得透透的，可享受这些美味时似乎丝毫不受妨碍。哈里·希思科特又喝了一小杯烧酒（nobblers）——这天才第二杯——然后就上床睡了。

## V Boscobel / V 波斯科贝尔

===Original===

Two days and two nights passed without fear of fire, and then Harry Heathcote was again on the alert. The earth was parched as though no drop of rain had fallen. The fences were dry as tinder, and the ground was strewed with broken atoms of timber from the trees, each of which a spark would ignite. Two nights Harry slept in his bed, but on the third he was on horseback about the run, watching, thinking, endeavouring to make provision, directing others, and hoping to make it believed that his eyes were everywhere. In this way an entire week was passed, and now it wanted but four days to Christmas. He would come home to breakfast about seven in the morning, very tired, but never owning that he was tired, and then sleep heavily for an hour or two in a chair. After that he would go out again on the run, would sleep perhaps for another hour after dinner, and then would start for his night’s patrol. During this week he saw nothing of Medlicot, and never mentioned his name but once. On that occasion his wife told him that during his absence Medlicot had been at the station.

“What brought him here?” Harry asked, fiercely.

Mrs. Heathcote explained that he had called in a friendly way, and had said that if there were any fear of fire he would be happy himself to lend assistance.

Then the young squatter forgot himself in his wrath. “Confound his hypocrisy!” said Harry, aloud.

“I don’t think he’s a hypocrite,” said the wife.

===Chinese===

两天两夜过去了，没有火灾的忧虑，随后哈里·希思科特又警觉起来。大地干得就好像一滴雨也不曾落过。篱笆干得像火绒，地上撒满了树上落下的碎木屑，一粒火星就能点着任何一片。有两个夜晚哈里睡在了床上，第三夜他又骑马巡行在牧场上，守望、思索、设法防备、调度人手，并想让人相信他的眼睛无处不在。就这样过了整整一个星期，如今离圣诞节只差四天了。他早晨七点左右回家吃早饭，疲惫不堪，却从不承认劳累，然后在椅子上沉沉睡上一两个钟头。之后他会再到牧场上去，午饭后或许再睡一个钟头，接着便出发进行夜间巡逻。这个星期里他没有见过梅德利科特，也只提过一次他的名字。那一次，他妻子告诉他，他不在家时梅德利科特来过场部。

“他来干什么？”哈里厉声问。

希思科特太太解释说，他是友好来访的，还说倘若有什么失火的危险，他本人很乐意出力相助。

这时年轻的牧场主怒不可遏，忘乎所以。“让他那副假仁假义见鬼去吧！”哈里大声说。

“我不觉得他是伪君子。”妻子说。

===Original===

“I’m sure he’s not,” said Kate Daly.

Not a word more was spoken, and Harry immediately left the house. The two women did not as usual go to the gate to see him mount his horse, not refraining from doing so in any anger, or as wishing to exhibit displeasure at Harry’s violence, but because they were afraid of him. They had found themselves compelled to differ from him, but were oppressed at finding themselves in opposition to him.

The feeling that his wife should in any way take part against him added greatly to Heathcote’s trouble. It produced in his mind a terrible feeling of loneliness in his sorrow. He bore a brave outside to all his men, and to any stranger whom in these days he met about the run﻿—to his wife and sister also, and to the old woman at home. He forced upon them all an idea that he was not only autocratic, but self-sufficient also﻿—that he wanted neither help nor sympathy. He never cried out in his pain, being heartily ashamed even of the appeal which he had made to Medlicot. He spoke aloud and laughed with the men, and never acknowledged that his trials were almost too much for him. But he was painfully conscious of his own weakness. He sometimes felt, when alone in the bush, that he would fain get off his horse, and lie upon the ground and weep till he slept. It was not that he trusted no one. He suspected no one with a positive suspicion, except Nokes, and Medlicot as the supporter of Nokes. But he had no one with whom he could converse freely, none whom he had not been accustomed to treat as the mere ministers of his will except his wife and his wife’s sister, and now he was disjoined from them by their sympathy with Medlicot! He had chosen to manage everything himself without contradiction and almost without counsel; but, like other such imperious masters, he now found that when trouble came the privilege of dictatorship brought with it an almost unsupportable burden.

Old Bates was an excellent man, of whose fidelity the young squatter was quite assured. No one understood foot-rot better than Old Bates, or was less sparing of himself in curing it. He was a second mother to all the lambs, and when shearing came watched with the eyes of Argus to see that the sheep were not wounded by the shearers, or the wool left on their backs. But he had no conversation, none of that imagination which in such a time as this might have assisted in devising safeguards, and but little enthusiasm. Shepherds, so called, Harry kept none upon the run; and would have felt himself insulted had anyone suggested that he was so backward in his ways as to employ men of that denomination. He had fenced his run, and dispensed with shepherds and shepherding as old-fashioned and unprofitable. He had two mounted men, whom he called boundary riders﻿—one an Irishman and the other a German﻿—and them he trusted fully, the German altogether, and the Irishman equally as regarded his honesty. But he could not explain to them the thoughts that loaded his brain. He could instigate them to eagerness; but he could not condescend to tell Karl Bender, the German, that if his fences were destroyed neither his means nor his credit would be sufficient to put them up again, and that if the scanty herbage were burnt off any large proportion of his run, he must sell his flocks at a great sacrifice. Nor could he explain to Mickey O’Dowd, the Irishman, that his peace of mind was destroyed by his fear of one man. He had to bear it all alone. And there was heavy on him also the great misery of feeling that everything might depend on own exertions, and that yet he did not know how or where to exert himself. When he had ridden about all night and discovered nothing, he might just as well have been in bed. And he was continually riding about all night and discovering nothing.

After leaving the station on the evening of the day on which he had expressed himself to the women so vehemently respecting Medlicot, he met Bates coming home from his day’s work. It was then past eight o’clock, and the old man was sitting wearily on his horse, with his head low down between his shoulders, and the reins hardly held within his grasp.

===Chinese===

“我敢肯定他不是。”凯特·戴利说。

再没有多说一句话，哈里随即离开了屋子。两个女人没有像往常那样到门口看他上马，倒不是因为生气而忍着不去，也不是想对哈里的粗暴表示不快，而是因为她们怕他。她们发觉自己不得不跟他意见相左，却又因为站到了他的对立面而心情沉重。

妻子竟会在任何一点上站到反对他的那一边去——这种滋味大大加重了希思科特的苦恼。这在他心中造成一种可怕的孤独感，令他悲哀。他对所有手下人、对这些天在牧场上遇到的任何生人，都摆出一副勇敢的外表——对妻子和小姨子如此，对家里的老妇人也是如此。他强使众人都相信，他不仅专断，而且自足——既不需要帮助，也不需要同情。他从不叫苦，甚至对自己向梅德利科特开口求助一事由衷地感到羞愧。他大声跟工人们谈笑，从不承认这些磨难几乎超出他的承受之力。可他痛苦地意识到自己的软弱。有时他独自在丛林里，真想跳下马，躺倒在地上，哭到睡着为止。这倒不是他不信任任何人。他没有明确怀疑过谁，只有诺克斯除外，还有作为诺克斯后台的梅德利科特。可是没有一个他能自由谈心的人，除了妻子和小姨子，他对谁都一向只当作执行他意志的差役看待，而如今她们却因为同情梅德利科特，跟他疏远了！他一向选择事事独断，不听异议，也几乎不纳忠言；可是像一切这样的专制主人一样，他如今发现，麻烦一来，独裁的特权便带来一副几乎扛不动的担子。

老贝茨是个极好的人，他的忠心，年轻的牧场主毫不怀疑。论羊腐蹄病的诊治，谁也不如老贝茨懂行，谁也不像他那样不惜气力。他简直是所有羊羔的第二个母亲，剪毛季节一到，他就以阿尔戈斯般的眼睛盯视着，不让羊被剪毛工弄伤，也不让羊毛留在羊背上。可他不善言谈，没有那种在这种时节能帮着出主意设防的想象力，也谈不上什么热忱。所谓牧羊人，哈里一个也不雇；谁要是暗示他的经营方式落伍到要雇那类名目的人手，他还会觉得受了侮辱。他给牧场圈了篱笆，把牧羊人和牧羊这一行当作过时而无利可图的旧规矩一并废除了。他有两名骑马的人手，他称之为巡栏员（boundary riders）——一个是爱尔兰人，一个是德国人——这两人他都完全信得过：德国人是整体上都信得过，爱尔兰人在诚实这一点上同样信得过。可他无法向他们倾诉压在心头的心事。他能煽起他们的热忱；却不肯屈尊告诉那德国人卡尔·本德：倘若篱笆被毁，他的财力也好、信用也好，都不足以再把篱笆立起来；倘若牧场有大片草皮被烧掉，他就只得忍痛贱卖羊群。他也无法向那爱尔兰人米基·奥多德（Mickey O’Dowd）解释：他的心境已被对一个人的恐惧搅得不得安宁。他只好独自扛着这一切。压在他身上的还有一桩大苦楚：一切都可能系于他个人的努力，可他又不知道该怎样用力、往哪里用力。夜里骑马跑了一圈又一无所获，那跟躺在床上又有什么两样。而他夜夜骑马巡行，夜夜一无所获。

在对两位妇人就梅德利科特的事激烈发表了一番议论的那天傍晚，他离开场部之后，遇见干完一天活回家的贝茨。那时已过八点，老人疲惫地坐在马上，脑袋低缩在两肩之间，缰绳几乎都握不住了。

===Original===

“You’re late, Mr. Bates,” said Harry; “you take too much out of yourself this hot weather.”

“I’ve got to move slower, Mr. Heathcote, as I grow older﻿—that’s about it﻿—and the beast I’m on is not much good.”

Now Mr. Bates was always complaining of his horse, and yet was allowed to choose any on the run for his own use.

“If you don’t like him, why don’t you take another?”

“There ain’t much difference in ’em, Mr. Heathcote. Better the devil you know than the devil you don’t. It’s getting uncommon close shaving for them wethers in the new paddock. They’re down upon the roots pretty well already.”

===Chinese===

“你回来得晚啦，贝茨先生，”哈里说，“这么热的天，你太不知保养自己了。”

“人老了，动作就得慢些，希思科特先生——就是这么回事——我骑的这头牲口也不顶用。”

话说贝茨先生老是抱怨自己的马，可牧场上的马随他挑哪一匹自用，从来都由他。

“你要是不喜欢它，干嘛不换一匹？”

“它们彼此也差不离，希思科特先生。熟悉的鬼总强过不熟悉的鬼。新围场里那些阉羊的日子越发紧巴了，草几乎已经啃到根了。”

===Original===

“There’s grass along the bush on the north side.”

“They won’t go there; it’s rank and sour. They won’t feed up there as long as they can live lower down and nearer the water. Weather like this, they’d sooner die near the water than travel to fill their bellies. It’s about the hottest day we’ve had, and the nights a’most hotter. Are you going to be out, Mr. Heathcote?”

“I think so.”

“What’s the good of it, Mr. Heathcote? There is no use in it. Lord love you, what can you do? You can’t be every side at once.”

“Fire can only travel with the wind, Mr. Bates.”

===Chinese===

“北边灌木林沿上还有草。”

“它们不肯去；那草又老又酸。只要在低处、离水近的地方还能活，它们就不肯到高地去吃草。这样的天气，它们宁可死在水边，也不肯跑远路填肚子。这差不多是咱们碰上过的最热的天了，夜里几乎更热。您今夜还要出去吗，希思科特先生？”

“我看要出去。”

“这有什么用呢，希思科特先生？没有用的。老天爷疼你，你能做什么呢？你总不能同时守住四面八方。”

“火只能顺风蔓延，贝茨先生。”

===Original===

“And there isn’t any wind, and so there can’t be any fire. I never did think, and I don’t think now, there ever was any use in a man fashing himself as you fash yourself. You can’t alter things, Mr. Heathcote.”

“But that’s just what I can do﻿—what a man has to do. If a match were thrown there at your feet, and the grass was aflame, couldn’t you alter that by putting your foot on it? If you find a ewe on her back, can’t you alter that by putting her on her legs?”

“Yes, I can do that, I suppose.”

“What does a man live for except to alter things? When a man clears the forest and sows corn, does he not alter things?”

“That’s not your line, Mr. Heathcote,” said the cunning old man.

===Chinese===

“眼下又没有风，所以也就不会有火。我从来就不觉得——如今也不觉得——一个人像您这样折磨自己有什么用处。人是改不了事情的，希思科特先生。”

“可那恰恰是我做得到的——也是人该做的。要是往你脚边扔一根火柴，草烧起来了，你一脚踩上去，不就把事改变了？要是你发现一只母羊四脚朝天，把它翻过来立起，不也把事改变了？”

“那个嘛，我想我做得到。”

“人活着不就是为了改变事情吗？一个人清了森林种上庄稼，难道不正是改变了事情？”

“那可不是您的行当，希思科特先生。”狡黠的老人说。

===Original===

“If I send wool to market, I alter things.”

“You’ll excuse me, Mr. Heathcote. Of course I’m old, but I just give you my experience.”

“I’m much obliged to you; though we can’t always agree, you know. Good night. Go in and say a word to my wife, and tell them you saw me all right.”

“I’ll have a crack with ’em, Mr. Heathcote, before I turn in.”

“And tell Mary I sent my love.”

===Chinese===

“我把羊毛送往市场，也是在改变事情。”

“您别见怪，希思科特先生。我固然老了，可我不过是把我的经验说给您听。”

“多谢你了；虽说你知道，咱们不一定总能说到一块儿。晚安。进去跟我妻子说句话，告诉她们你见我好好的。”

“我上床前跟她们聊上一会儿，希思科特先生。”

“再告诉玛丽，说我问她好。”

===Original===

“I will, Mr. Heathcote; I will.”

He was thinking always of his wife during his solitary rides, and of her fear and deep anxiety. It was for her sake and for the children that he was so careworn﻿—not for his own. Had he been alone in the world he would not have fretted himself in this fashion because of the malice of any man. But how would it be with her should he be forced to move her from Gangoil? And yet, with all his love, they had parted almost in anger. Surely she would understand the tenderness of the message he had just sent her?

Of a sudden, as he was riding, he stopped his horse and listened attentively. From a great distance there fell upon his accustomed ear a sound which he recognized, though he was aware that the place from whence it came was at least two miles distant. It was the thud of an axe against a tree. He listened still, and was sure that it was so, and turned at once toward the sound, though in doing so he left his course at a right angle. He had been going directly away from the river, with his back to the wool-shed; but now he changed his course, riding in the direction of the spot at which Jacko had nearly fallen in jumping over the fence. As he continued on, the sounds became plainer, till at last, reining in his horse, he could see the form of the woodman, who was still at work ringing the trees. This was a job which the man did by contract, receiving so much an acre for the depopulation of the timber. It was now bright moonlight, almost as clear as day﻿—a very different night, indeed, from that on which the rain had come﻿—and Harry could see at a glance that it was the man called Boscobel still at work. Now there were, as he thought, very good reasons why Boscobel at the present moment should not be so employed. Boscobel was receiving wages for work of another kind.

“Bos,” said the squatter, riding up, and addressing the man by the customary abbreviation of his nickname, “I thought you were watching at Brownbie’s boundary?” Boscobel lowered his axe, and stood for a while contemplating the proposition made to him. “You are drawing three shillings a night for watching; isn’t that so?”

“Yes, that’s so. Anyways, I shall draw it.”

===Chinese===

“我会的，希思科特先生；我会的。”

独自骑行的路上，他心里始终惦着妻子，惦着她的恐惧和深深的忧虑。他这般操劳忧煎，是为了她，为了孩子们——不是为了自己。倘若他孤身一人活在世上，断不会因为哪个人的恶意就这样折磨自己。可万一他被迫带她离开冈戈伊尔，她会怎样呢？然而他这么爱她，两人却几乎是赌着气分开的。她总该领会他刚才捎去的那句话里的柔情吧？

他正骑行间，忽然勒住马，凝神细听。一阵熟悉的声响从远处传来，落进他那听惯了的耳朵——他辨出那是什么，虽然明知发声之地至少在两英里外。那是斧子砍树的咚咚声。他又听了一阵，确信无误，便立刻朝声音转去，虽然这一转，他的路线偏成了直角。他本来正径直背离河流而行，背对着剪毛棚；此刻却改了道，朝贾科跳篱笆时差点摔下来的那块地方驰去。一路行去，声响愈来愈清楚，最后他勒住马，已能望见伐木工的身影，那人还在环剥树皮（ringing the trees）干活。这活计那人是包工做的，按每英亩若干工钱，替这片林木“清户”。此时月光明亮，几乎亮如白昼——的确，同落雨那夜已是全然不同的光景——哈里一眼便看出，还在干活的正是那个被人唤作波斯科贝尔（Boscobel）的人。而在他看来，眼下这个当口，波斯科贝尔实在不该在这里干这种活。波斯科贝尔领着另一种活计的工钱。

“博斯，”牧场主策马上前，用那人绰号惯常的简称唤道，“我还当你在布朗比家边界上守夜呢？”波斯科贝尔放下斧子，站着琢磨了片刻对他说的这番话。“你守夜每晚领三先令，是不是这样？”

“是，是这样。反正那钱我是要领的。”

===Original===

“Then why ain’t you watching?”

“There’s nothing to watch that I knows on﻿—not just now.”

“Then why should I pay you for it? I’m to pay you for ringing these trees, ain’t I?”

“Certainly, Mr. Heathcote.”

“Then you’re to make double use of your time, and sell it twice over, are you? Don’t try to look like a fool, as though you didn’t understand. You know that what you’re doing isn’t honest.”

===Chinese===

“那你为什么不去守？”

“据我所知，没啥可守的——眼下没有。”

“那我凭什么付这笔钱？我还要付你剥这些树皮的工钱，是不是？”

“那当然，希思科特先生。”

“这么说，你是要把时间一材两用，一份卖两回，是不是？别装傻，好像你听不懂似的。你清楚你干的这事不老实。”

===Original===

“Nobody ever said as I wasn’t honest before.”

“I tell you so now. You’re robbing me of the time you’ve sold to me, and for which I’m to pay you.”

“There ain’t nothing to watch while the wind’s as it is now, and that chap ain’t anywhere about tonight.”

“What chap?”

“Oh, I know. I’m all right. What’s the use of dawdling about up there in the broad moonlight, and the wind like this?”

===Chinese===

“从前可没人说过我不老实。”

“我现在就这么说你。你偷盗了你已经卖给我、要我付钱的那段时间。”

“眼下风这样，没啥可守的，那家伙今儿晚上也没在这一带转。”

“哪个家伙？”

“哦，我知道。我心中有数。大月亮地里，风又这样，在上头那儿磨蹭个啥劲儿？”

===Original===

“That’s for me to judge. If you engage to do my work and take my money, you’re swindling me when you go about another job as you are now. You needn’t scratch your head. You understand it all as well as I do.”

“I never was told I swindled before, and I ain’t a-going to put up with it. You may ring your own trees, and watch your own fences, and the whole place may be burned for me. I ain’t a-going to do another turn in Gangoil. Swindle, indeed!” So Boscobel shouldered his axe, and marched off through the forest, visible in the moonlight till the trees hid him.

There was another enemy made! He had never felt quite sure of this man, but had been glad to have him about the place as being thoroughly efficient in his own business. It was only during the last ten days that he had agreed to pay him for night-watching, leaving the man to do as much additional day-work as he pleased, for which, of course, he would be paid at the regular contract price. There was a double purpose intended in this watching, as was well understood by all the hands employed; first, that of preventing incendiary fire by the mere presence of the watchers, and, secondly, that of being at hand to extinguish fire in case of need. Now a man ringing trees five or six miles away from the beat on which he was stationed could not serve either of these purposes. Boscobel therefore had been fraudulently at work for his own dishonest purposes, and knew well that his employment was of that nature. All this was quite clear to Heathcote; and it was clear to him, also, that when he detected fraud he was bound to expose it. Had the man acknowledged his fault and been submissive, there would have been an end of the matter. Heathcote would have said no word about it to anyone, and would not have stopped a farthing from the week’s unearned wages. That he had to encounter a certain amount of ill usage from the rough men about him, and to forgive it, he could understand; but it could not be his duty, either as a man or a master, to pass over dishonesty without noticing it. No; that he would not do, though Gangoil should burn from end to end. He did not much mind being robbed. He knew that to a certain extent he must endure to be cheated﻿—he would endure it﻿—but he would never teach his men to think that he passed over such matters because he was afraid of them, or that dishonesty on their part was indifferent to him.

But now he had made another enemy﻿—an enemy of a man who had declared to him that he knew the movements of “that chap,” meaning Nokes! How hard the world was! It seemed that all around were trouble to him. He turned his horse back, and made again for the spot which was his original destination. As he cantered on among the trees, twisting here and there, and regulating his way by the stars, he asked himself whether it would not be better for him to go home and lay himself down by his wife and sleep, and await the worst that these men could do to him. This idea was so strong upon him that at one spot he made his horse stop till he had thought it all out. No one encouraged him in his work. Everyone about the place, friend or foe, Bates, his wife, Medlicot, and this Boscobel, spoke to him as though he were fussy and fidgety in his anxiety. “If fires must come, they will come; and if they are not to come, you are simply losing your labour.” This was the upshot of all they said to him. Why should he be wiser than they? If the ruin came, let it come. Old Bates had been ruined, but still had enough to eat and drink, and clothes to wear, and did not work half as hard as his employer. He thought that if he could only find some one person who would sympathize with him and support him, he would not mind. But the mental loneliness of his position almost broke his heart.

===Chinese===

“那轮得着我来判断。你要是应承了我的活计、拿了我的钱，却像现在这样去干别的营生，那就是在欺骗我。用不着挠头。这里头的道理你跟我一样明白。”

“从来没人说过我欺骗谁，这事我不能受着。您自个儿剥自个儿的树，自个儿守自个儿的篱笆吧，整块地方烧个精光也不关我的事。冈戈伊尔的活儿我是一件也不干了。还欺骗呢！”说罢，波斯科贝尔背起斧子，穿过森林扬长而去，月光下望得见他的背影，直到树木把他遮没。

这下又结下了一个仇敌！这个人他向来没有十分把握，但很乐意把他留在场上，因为他在自己那行当里十分在行。就在前十来天里，他才说好付他守夜的工钱，任他白天再随意揽多少额外的活计——那些活计自然按常规包工价付酬。这守夜是一举两用，雇工们心里都明白：其一，守夜人在场，本身就能吓阻纵火；其二，一旦有失火的动静，好就近扑灭。可一个人跑到离他值守的地段五六英里之外去剥树皮，这两个用途就一样也谈不上了。所以波斯科贝尔一直是在昧着良心给自己捞钱，而且他清楚自己干的活是什么性质。这一切希思科特看得分明；他同样看得分明：查出了欺瞒，就有责任予以揭穿。倘若那人认了错、服个软，这事也就了结了。希思科特不会对任何人提一个字，那一个星期没干活的白饷也一分不少地照付。周围粗人来些欺辱，他得受着、得宽恕，这他懂得；可是对不老实的行为视若无睹，无论作为一个人还是作为东家，都不可能是他的本分。不；这个他不干，哪怕冈戈伊尔从这头烧到那头。他并不怎么在乎被偷窃。他知道在一定程度上他必须忍着受骗——他会忍——可他绝不能让手下人以为，他放过这类事情是因为怕他们，以为他们不老实他也满不在乎。

可是如今他又结了一个仇——结在一个曾经向他声言自己知道“那家伙”行踪的人身上，那家伙指的就是诺克斯！这世道多么难哪！他四下里似乎处处是烦难。他拨转马头，重新朝原先的目的地走去。他在林木间策马小跑，东拐西绕，凭着星星辨向，一路自问：不如回家去，躺到妻子身边睡下，听凭这些人把最坏的招数使出来，岂不更好？这个念头如此强烈，有一处他竟勒住马，把前后都想了个透。没有人给他打气。这地方上上下下，无论敌友，贝茨、他妻子、梅德利科特，还有这个波斯科贝尔，说起话来都好像他是急惊风似的多虑瞎忙。“火要是注定要来，它总会来；要是不会来，你不过是白费力气。”这就是他们众口一词的要旨。他凭什么要比他们高明？毁灭要来，就让它来。老贝茨当年也破过产，如今不还是有吃有喝、有衣裳穿，干活还不及东家的一半苦。他想，只要能找到一个人同情他、支持他，他也就无所谓了。可是他处境中那种精神上的孤独，几乎叫他心碎。

===Original===

Then there came across his mind the dim remembrance of certain old school words, and he touched his horse with his spur and hurried onward: “Let there be no steps backward.” A thought as to the manliness of persevering, of the want of manliness in yielding to depression, came to his rescue. Let him, at any rate, have the comfort of thinking that he had done his best according to his lights. After some dim fashion, he did come to recognize it as a fact that nothing could really support him but self-approbation. Though he fell from his horse in utter weariness, he would persevere.

As the night wore on he came to the German’s hut, and finding it empty, as he expected, rode on to the outside fence of his run. When he reached this he got off his horse, and taking a key out of his pocket, whistled upon it loudly. A few minutes afterward the German came up to him.

“There’s been no one about, I suppose?” he asked.

“Not a one,” said the man.

“You’ve been across on Brownbie’s run?”

===Chinese===

这时，学生时代的几句老话模模糊糊浮上心头，他用马刺催了催马，急急赶路：“绝不后退半步。”一念及坚持到底才是丈夫气概、向沮丧屈服便是丧失丈夫气概，这想法救了他。至少，让他存着一份安慰：自己已按心中的光亮尽了全力。朦朦胧胧之间，他确实认识到一个事实：真正能支撑他的，唯有自我的认可。哪怕累得从马上栽下去，他也要坚持。

夜深了，他来到德国人的小屋，见屋里果然空着，便继续骑到牧场外围的篱笆。到了那里他下了马，从口袋里掏出一把钥匙，衔在唇边吹出很响的哨音。几分钟后，德国人来到他跟前。

“没见有人来过吧，我想？”他问。

“一个也没有。”那人说。

“你到布朗比家的牧场上看过？”

===Original===

“Ve’re on it now, Mr. ’Eathcote.” They were both on the side of the fence away from Gangoil station.

“I don’t know how that is, Karl. I think Gangoil goes a quarter of a mile beyond this. But we did not quite strike the boundary when we put up the fence.”

“Brownbie’s cattle is allays here, Mr. ’Eathcote, and is knocking down the fence every day. Brownbie is a rascal, and ’is cattle as bad as ’isself.”

“Never mind that, Karl, now. When we’ve got through the heats, we’ll put a mile or two of better fencing along here. You know Boscobel?”

“In course I know Bos.”

===Chinese===

“咱们这会儿就在上头呢，希思科特先生。”两人这时都在篱笆上背离冈戈伊尔场部的那一侧。

“这就不知道怎么说的了，卡尔。我记得冈戈伊尔的地界还要往外伸四分之一英里。当初立篱笆时，我们没有正好卡在边界线上。”

“布朗比家的牛老在这儿，希思科特先生，天天把篱笆撞倒。布朗比是个无赖，他的牛跟他一样坏。”

“那个先别管，卡尔。等这阵酷热熬过去，咱们沿这一带修上一两英里像样些的篱笆。你认识波斯科贝尔吧？”

“当然认识博斯。”

===Original===

“What sort of a fellow is he?” Then Harry told his German dependent exactly what had taken place between him and the other man.

“He’s in and in wid all them young Brownbies,” said Karl.

“The Brownbies are a bad lot, but I don’t think they’d do anything of this kind,” said Harry, whose mind was still dwelling on the dangers of fire.

“They likes muttons, Mr. ’Eathcote.”

“I suppose they do take a sheep or two now and then. They wouldn’t do worse than that, would they?”

===Chinese===

“他是个什么样的人？”接着，哈里把他和那人之间发生的事，原原本本讲给他这相依的德国人听。

“他跟那帮小布朗比们打得火热。”卡尔说。

“布朗比家是一伙坏人，可我不认为他们会干这种事。”哈里说，他心里仍在惦着火灾的危险。

“他们爱吃羊肉，希思科特先生。”

“我看他们隔三差五也确实偷个一只两只羊。他们不至于干比这更狠的，会吗？”

===Original===

“Noting too ’ot for ’em; noting too ’eavy,” said Karl, smoking his pipe. “The vind, vat there is, comes just here, Mr. ’Eathcote.” And the man lifted up his arm, and pointed across in the direction of Brownbie’s run.

“And you don’t think much of Boscobel?” Karl Bender shook his head. “He was always well treated here,” said Harry, “and has had plenty of work, and earned large wages. The man will be a fool to quarrel with me.” Karl again shook his head. With Karl Bender, Harry was quite sure of his man, but not on that account need he be quite sure of the correctness of the man’s opinion.

Thence he went on till he met his other lieutenant, O’Dowd; and so, having completed his work, he made his way home, reaching the station at sunrise.

“Did Bates tell you he’d met me?” he asked his wife.

“Yes, Harry; kiss me, Harry! I was so glad you sent a word. Promise me, Harry, not to think that I don’t agree with you in everything.”

===Chinese===

“对他们来说没啥太烫的，也没啥太沉的。”卡尔抽着烟斗说。“那点风，就这么点儿风，恰恰是从这儿过，希思科特先生。”说着那人抬起手臂，朝布朗比家牧场的方向指过去。

“这么说你不看好波斯科贝尔？”卡尔·本德摇了摇头。“他在这里一向待遇不错，”哈里说，“活计多的是，挣的工钱不少。这人跟我闹翻，就是个傻瓜。”卡尔又摇了摇头。对卡尔·本德，哈里对这个人本身是放心的，但也不能因此就认定这人的判断一定对。

随后他继续前行，直到遇见他另一位副手奥多德；就这样，公事办完，他便往家走，日出时分到了场部。

“贝茨跟你说他碰见我了吗？”他问妻子。

“说了，哈里；亲亲我，哈里！你捎话来，我高兴极了。答应我，哈里，别以为我在任何事情上不同意你。”

## XII Conclusion / XII 结局

===Original===

The constables had started from Gangoil, on their way to Boolabong, a little after four, and from that time till he was made to get out of bed for his dinner Harry Heathcote was allowed to sleep. He had richly earned his rest by his work, and he lay motionless, without a sound, in the broad daylight, with his arm under his head﻿—dreaming, no doubt, of some happy squatting land, in which there were no free-selectors, no fires, no rebellious servants, no floods, no droughts, no wild dogs to worry the lambs, no grass seeds to get into the fleeces, and in which the price of wool stood steady at two shillings and sixpence a pound. His wife from time to time came into the room, shading the light from his eyes, protecting him from the flies, and administering in her soft way to what she thought might be his comforts. His sleep was of the kind which no light, nor even flies, can interrupt. Once or twice she stooped down and kissed his brow, but he was altogether unconscious of her caress.

During this time old Mrs. Medlicot arrived; but her coming did not awake the sleeper, though it was by no means made in silence. The old woman sobbed and cried over her son, at the same time expressing her thankfulness that he should have turned up in the forest so exactly at the proper moment﻿—evidently taking part in the conviction that her Giles had saved Gangoil and all its sheep. And then there were all the necessary arrangements to be made for the night, in accordance with which almost everybody had to give up his or her bed and sleep somewhere else. But nothing disturbed Harry. For the present he was allowed to occupy his own room, and he enjoyed the privilege.

Kate Daly during this time was much disturbed in mind. The reader may remember﻿—Kate, at any rate, remembered well﻿—that, just as the doctor had arrived to set his broken bone, Mr. Medlicot, disabled as he was, had attempted to take her by the arm. He had certainly chosen an odd time for a declaration of love, just the moment in which he ought to have been preparing himself for the manipulation of his fractured limb; but, unless he had meant a declaration of love, surely he would not have seized her by the arm. It was a matter to her of great moment. Oh, of what vital importance! The English girl living in a town﻿—or even in what we call the country﻿—has no need to think of any special man till some special man thinks of her. Men are fairly plentiful, and if one man does not come, another will. And there have probably been men coming and going in some sort since the girl left her schoolroom and became a young lady. But in the bush the thing is very different. It may be that there is no young man available within fifty miles﻿—no possible lover or future husband, unless Heaven should interfere almost with a miracle. To those to whom lovers are as plentiful as blackberries it may seem indelicate to surmise that the thought of such a want should ever enter a girl’s head. I doubt whether the defined idea of any want had ever entered poor Kate’s head. But now that the possible lover was there﻿—not only possible, but very probable﻿—and so eligible in many respects, living so close, with a house over his head and a good business; and then so handsome, and, as Kate thought, so complete a gentleman! Of course she turned it much in her mind. She was very happy with Harry Heathcote. There never was a brother-in-law so good! But, after all, what is a brother-in-law, though he be the very best? Kate had already begun to fancy that a house of her own and a husband of her own would be essential to her happiness. But then a man can not be expected to make an offer with a broken collarbone﻿—certainly can not do so just when the doctor has arrived to set the bone.

Late on in the day, when the doctor had gone, and Medlicot was, according to instructions, sitting out on the veranda in an armchair, and his mother was with him, and while Harry was sleeping as though he never meant to be awake again, Kate managed to say a few words to her sister. It will be understood that the ladies’ hands were by no means empty. The Christmas dinner was in course of preparation, and Sing Sing, that villainous Chinese cook, had absconded. Mrs. Growler, no doubt, did her best; but Mrs. Growler was old and slow, and the house was full of guests. It was by no means an idle time; but still Kate found an opportunity to say a word to her sister in the kitchen.

“What do you think of him, Mary?”

===Chinese===

警察们从冈戈伊尔动身前往布拉邦是四点刚过的事，从那时起，直到被叫起来吃晚饭，哈里·希思科特一直获准睡觉。他出的力足以换来这顿休息，他一动不动、悄无声息地躺在朗朗白昼里，手臂枕在头下——无疑正梦见某片幸福太平的牧场乐土：那里没有自由选地农，没有火灾，没有不听话的仆役，没有洪水，没有旱灾，没有糟蹋羊羔的野狗，没有钻进羊毛的草籽，而且羊毛的价钱稳稳地站在每磅两先令六便士上。他的妻子不时走进房间，替他遮着眼里的光，替他驱赶苍蝇，用她温柔的方式照料着她认为能让他舒服的一切。他这一觉睡得连光线、连苍蝇都搅扰不动。有一两回她俯身吻他的额头，可他对她的爱抚浑然不觉。

这当口，梅德利科特老太太到了；她的到来并没有惊醒睡者，虽说一路上绝非悄无声息。老太太在儿子身上又哭又泣，一面又千恩万谢，庆幸他恰好在节骨眼上分毫不差地出现在森林里——显然她也认定，是她的吉尔斯救下了冈戈伊尔和全部的羊。接着又是一应过夜的安排要料理，照这安排，几乎人人都得让出自己的床，到别处去睡。可什么也搅扰不了哈里。眼下他获准占用自家的房间，他也乐得享受这份特权。

这段时间里，凯特·戴利心绪颇为不宁。读者也许还记得——凯特无论如何记得清楚——大夫刚到、正要给那断骨正位的时候，梅德利科特先生不顾自身伤残，曾想拉她的胳膊。他挑来表白爱情的时候实在古怪，恰是本该凝神准备让人摆弄断肢的当口；可倘若不是存心求爱，他总不至于伸手去抓她的胳膊。这对她是件天大的事。啊，何等性命攸关！住在城镇里的英国姑娘——哪怕住在我们所称的乡间——用不着惦记哪个特别的男人，直到哪个特别的男人惦记上她。男人相当多，这一个不来，自会有另一个来。而且自打姑娘走出学堂、长成少妇，多半早就有各色男人来来去去。可在丛林里，情形全然不同。方圆五十英里内未必寻得着一个年轻男人——寻不着可能的情人、未来的丈夫，除非上苍行个近乎奇迹的方便。在情人们多如黑莓的人看来，猜想一个姑娘竟会动这种缺憾的念头，未免有失体统。我怀疑，这缺憾的明确念头是否真曾钻进过可怜的凯特的脑子。可如今那可能的情人就在眼前——不但可能，而且大有指望——而且在许多方面都这般合适：住得这样近，有自己的房子，有兴旺的营生；人又生得俊，而且在凯特看来，是那般十全十美的绅士！她自然在心里翻来覆去地想。她在哈里·希思科特家过得很幸福。再没有更好的姐夫了！可是说到底，姐夫就算再好，终究只是个姐夫。凯特已经渐渐觉得，一栋属于自己的房子、一个属于自己的丈夫，才是她幸福之所必需。只是总不能指望一个男人顶着一根断锁骨求婚——更不能恰恰在大夫上门正骨的当口求。

这天晚些时候，大夫走了，梅德利科特遵着医嘱坐在游廊的扶手椅里，他母亲陪着他；哈里则睡得仿佛再也不打算醒来。凯特寻机跟姐姐说了几句话。要知道，两位女士的手可绝不是闲着的。圣诞大餐正在操办，那个恶棍般的中国厨子辛辛（Sing Sing）又卷逃了。格罗勒太太（Mrs. Growler）无疑已尽了力；可格罗勒太太人老手慢，屋里又满是客人。这实在不是个清闲的时辰；但凯特还是在厨房里找到了跟姐姐说话的机会。

“你觉得他这人怎么样，玛丽？”

===Original===

To the married sister “him” would naturally mean Harry Heathcote, of whom, as he lay asleep, the young wife thought that he was the very perfection of patriarchal pastoral manliness; but she knew enough of human nature to be aware that the “him” of the moment to her sister was no longer her own husband.

“I think he has got his arm broken fighting for Harry, and that we are bound to do the best we can for him.”

“Oh yes; that’s of course. I’m sure Harry will feel that. He used, you know, to﻿—to﻿—that is, not just to like him, because he is a free-selector.”

“They’ll drop all that now. Of course they could not be expected to know each other at the first starting. I shouldn’t wonder if they became regular friends.”

“That would be nice! After all, though you may be so happy at home, it is better to have something like a neighbour. Don’t you think so?”

===Chinese===

对已婚的姐姐来说，“他”自然是指哈里·希思科特；他睡着的时候，年轻的主妇觉得他正是家长式牧人男子气概的完美化身；但她也颇通人情，知道此刻妹妹口中的“他”，已不再是自己的丈夫。

“我觉得他是为哈里打架才断的胳膊，咱们理当尽心尽力照顾他。”

“哦，那是当然。我相信哈里也会这么想。你要知道，他从前——从前——就是说，谈不上喜欢他，因为他是自由选地农。”

“那些现在都会撂开了。头一回打交道，当然不能指望彼此了解。往后他们要是成了地道的伙伴，我一点也不奇怪。”

“那可太好了！说到底，家里就算再幸福，有个像样的邻居总归好些。你说是不是？”

===Original===

“It depends on who the neighbours are. I don’t care much for the Brownbies.”

“They are quite different, Mary.”

“I like the Medlicots very much.”

“I consider he’s quite a gentleman,” said Kate.

“Of course he’s a gentleman. Look here, Kate﻿—I shall be ready to welcome Mr. Medlicot as a brother-in-law, if things should turn out that way.”

===Chinese===

“那得看邻居是谁。我可不怎么喜欢布朗比家。”

“他们完全是两路人，玛丽。”

“我很喜欢梅德利科特家的人。”

“我看他完全是个绅士。”凯特说。

“他当然是绅士。听我说，凯特——倘若事情真到了那一步，我会乐意迎接梅德利科特先生做我的妹夫。”

===Original===

“I didn’t mean that, Mary.”

“Did you not? Well, you can mean it if you please, as far as I am concerned. Has he said anything to you, dear?”

“No.”

“Not a word?”

“I don’t know what you call a word; not a word of that kind.”

===Chinese===

“我不是那个意思，玛丽。”

“不是吗？好吧，就我这边而论，你尽可以是那个意思。他跟你说过什么没有，亲爱的？”

“没有。”

“一句也没说？”

“我不知道你所谓的‘一句’算什么；那一类的话一句也没有。”

===Original===

“I thought, perhaps﻿—”

“I think he meant it once﻿—this morning.”

“I dare say he meant it. And if he meant it this morning, he won’t have forgotten his meaning tomorrow.”

“There’s no reason why he should mean it, you know.”

“None in the least, Kate, is there?”

===Chinese===

“我还当，也许——”

“我想他有一回是这个意思——就在今天早上。”

“我敢说他是有那个意思。他今天早上既有那个意思，明天就不会忘了。”

“你要知道，他也没有理由非有那个意思不可。”

“一点理由也没有，凯特，是吗？”

===Original===

“Now you’re laughing at me, Mary. I never used to laugh at you when Harry was coming. I was so glad, and I did everything I could.”

“Yes, you went away and left us in the Botanical Gardens. I remember. But, you see, there are no Botanical Gardens here; and the poor man couldn’t walk about if there were.”

“I wonder what Harry would say if it were to be so.”

“Of course he’d be glad, for your sake.”

“But he does so despise free-selectors! And then he used to think that Mr. Medlicot was quite as bad as the Brownbies. I wouldn’t marry anyone to be despised by you and Harry.”

===Chinese===

“你这可是笑话我了，玛丽。当初哈里追你的时候，我可从没笑话过你。我高兴还来不及，能做的我都做了。”

“可不是，你把我们俩撂在植物园里自己走了。我记得。可你瞧，这儿没有植物园；就算有，那位可怜的人也走不动啊。”

“真要到了那一步，不知哈里会说什么。”

“他当然会高兴，为了你的缘故。”

“可他太看不起自由选地农了！而且他从前一直觉得梅德利科特先生跟布朗比家一样坏。我可不愿意嫁个让你们和哈里瞧不起的人。”

===Original===

“That’s all gone by, my dear,” said the wife, feeling that she had to apologize for her husband’s prejudices. “Of course one has to find out what people are before one takes them to one’s bosom. Mr. Medlicot has acted in the most friendly way about these fires, and I’m sure Harry will never despise him any more.”

“He couldn’t have done more for a real brother than have his arm broken.”

“But you must remember one thing, Kate, Mr. Medlicot is very nice, and like a gentleman, and all that; but you never can be quite certain about any man till he speaks out plainly. Don’t set your heart upon him till you are quite sure that he has set his upon you.”

“Oh no!” said Kate, giving her maidenly assurance when it was so much too late! Just at this moment Mrs. Growler came into the kitchen, and Kate’s promises and her sister’s cautions were for the moment silenced.

“How we’re to manage to get the dinner on the table, I for one don’t know at all,” said Mrs. Growler. “There’s Mr. Bates’ll be here; that will be six of ’em; and that Mr. Medlicot will want somebody to do everything for him, because he’s been and got hisself smashed. And there’s the old lady has just come out from home, and is as particular as anything. And Mr. Harry himself never thinks of things at all. One pair of hands, and them very old, can’t do everything for everybody.” All of which was very well understood to mean nothing at all.

===Chinese===

“那些都过去了，亲爱的。”主妇说，觉得该替丈夫的偏见赔个不是。“人当然得先摸清底细，再引为至亲。这几场火的事上，梅德利科特先生行事极够朋友，我敢肯定哈里往后再也不会看不起他了。”

“便是亲兄弟，也不过替人断条胳膊到这一步了。”

“可有一件事你得记住，凯特：梅德利科特先生人是极好的，有绅士派头，样样都好；可任何男人，不等到他把话明明白白说出口，你永远不能十分拿得准。别先把心放在他身上，除非你十分有把握他已把心放在你身上。”

“哦，才不会呢！”凯特说，献上她的少女保证——可惜为时太晚了！正在这时，格罗勒太太走进厨房，凯特的承诺和姐姐的告诫一时都被打断了。

“这顿饭怎么弄上桌，我是一点数都没有。”格罗勒太太说。“贝茨先生要来；这一下就是六个人；那位梅德利科特先生呢，样样都得有人伺候，因为他把自己给摔散了架。还有那位老太太，刚从国内出来，讲究得不得了。哈里先生自己呢，什么事都想不到。一双手，还是双老手，总不能替人人做尽一切。”这一大通话，大家都听得明明白白，其实等于什么也没说。

===Original===

Household deficiencies﻿—and, indeed, all deficiencies﻿—are considerable or insignificant in accordance with the aspirations of those concerned. When a man has a regiment of servants in his dining-room, with beautifully cut glass, a forest of flowers, and an iceberg in the middle of his table if the weather be hot, his guests will think themselves ill used and badly fed if aught in the banquet be astray. There must not be a rose leaf ruffled; a failure in the attendance, a falling off in a dish, or a fault in the wine is a crime. But the same guests shall be merry as the evening is long with a leg of mutton and whisky toddy, and will change their own plates, and clear their own table, and think nothing wrong, if from the beginning such has been the intention of the giver of the feast. In spite of Mrs. Growler’s prognostications, though the cook had absconded, and the chief guest of the occasion could not cut up his own meat, that Christmas dinner at Gangoil was eaten with great satisfaction.

Harry had been so far triumphant. He had stopped the fire that was intended to ruin him, he had beaten off his enemies on their own ground, and he was no longer oppressed by that sense of desolation which had almost overpowered him.

“We’ll give one toast, Mrs. Medlicot,” he said, when Mrs. Growler and Kate between them had taken away the relics of the plum-pudding. “Our friends at home!”

The poor lady drank the toast with a sob. “That’s vera weel for you, Mr. Heathcote. You’re young, and will win your way hame, and see auld friends again, nae doubt; but I’ll never see ane of them mair, except those I have here.” Nevertheless, the old lady ate her dinner and drank her toddy, and made much of the occasion, going in and out to her son upon the veranda.

===Chinese===

家务上的短缺——其实一切短缺都如此——其轻重全看当事人的志趣。一个人的餐室里若有一队仆人、精美雕花的玻璃器皿、一丛鲜花，天热时桌子中央还搁着一座冰山，宴席上稍有差池，客人便会觉得受了亏待、吃得不称心。玫瑰花瓣一片也不许乱；上菜慢了、菜肴差了、酒有了毛病，都是罪过。可同样是这些客人，一条羊腿加威士忌潘趣酒，也能欢欢喜喜闹一晚上，自己换盘子、自己收拾桌子，不觉得有任何不妥——只要设宴的主人打一开始就是这个打算。尽管格罗勒太太作了那些凶险的预言，尽管厨子卷逃了、席上的主宾连自己的肉都切不了，冈戈伊尔这顿圣诞大餐还是吃得人人满意。

哈里到目前为止是大获全胜了。他扑灭了那场存心要毁掉他的大火，在敌人的地盘上打退了他们，那种几乎把他压垮的凄惶之感也不再来折磨他了。

“咱们来敬一杯酒，梅德利科特太太。”等格罗勒太太和凯特合力撤下葡萄干布丁的残盘，他说道。“敬国内的朋友们！”

可怜的太太饮下这杯祝酒，带着一声呜咽。“这话对您是挺好说的，希思科特先生。您年轻，总有一天挣得着回乡的路，不消说还能再见着老朋友；可我呀，再也见不着他们哪一个喽，除了眼前身边这几个。”话虽如此，老太太照样吃了饭，喝了潘趣酒，把这节庆办得有模有样，还一趟趟进出，到游廊上去看儿子。

===Original===

Soon after dinner Heathcote, as was his wont, strayed out with his prime minister Bates to consult on the dangers which might be supposed still to threaten his kingdom, and Mrs. Heathcote, with her youngest boy in her lap, sat talking to Mrs. Medlicot in the parlour. Such was not her custom in weather such as this. Kate had been sent out on to the veranda, with special commands to attend to the wants of the sufferer, and Mrs. Heathcote would have followed her had she not remembered her sister’s appeal, “I did everything I could for you.” In those happy days Kate had been very good, and certainly deserved requital for her services. And therefore, when the men had gone out, Mrs. Heathcote, with her guest, remained in the warm room, and went so far as to suggest that at that period of the day the room was preferable to the veranda. Poor Mrs. Medlicot was new to the ways of the bush, and fell into the trap; thus Kate Daly was left alone with her wounded hero.

When told to take him out his glass of wine, and when conscious that no one followed her, she felt herself to have been guilty of some great sin, and was almost tempted to escape. She had asked her sister for help; and this was the help that was forthcoming﻿—help so palpable, so manifest, as to be almost indelicate. Would he think that plans were being made to catch him, now that he was a captive and impotent? The thought that it was possible that such an idea might occur to him was terrible to her. She would rather lose him altogether than feel the stain of such a suggestion on her own conscience. She put the glass of wine down on the little table by his side, and then attempted to withdraw.

“Stay a moment with me,” he said. “Where are they all?”

“Mary and your mother are inside. Harry and Mr. Bates have gone across to look at the horses.”

“I almost feel as though I could walk, too.”

===Chinese===

饭后不久，希思科特照他的老习惯，同他的宰相贝茨踱到外头，商议那些想来仍会威胁他王国的隐患；希思科特太太抱着最小的男孩，坐在客厅里陪梅德利科特太太说话。这样的天气她本不惯如此。凯特被打发到游廊上，受了特意的嘱托，去伺候那位伤员的种种需要；希思科特太太本想跟着出去，若不是记起妹妹那句“我可是能做的都做了”。当年那些快活日子里凯特出过大力，她的辛劳理应得到报偿。于是男人们出去之后，希思科特太太便陪着客人留在暖和的屋里，甚至进一步点明：一天中的这个时辰，屋里比游廊舒服。可怜的梅德利科特太太初来乍到，不懂丛林里的门道，落进了圈套；就这样，凯特·戴利单独同她负伤的英雄待在了一起。

奉命给他端酒出去，又发觉没有人跟出来，她觉得自己像是犯下了什么大罪，几乎想转身逃走。她本是求姐姐帮忙的；可等来的竟是这样的帮忙——这样露骨、这样明白的帮忙，简直近乎不体面。他会怎么想？会不会以为自己如今成了俘虏、动弹不得，人家就设好了圈套捉他？一想到他心里可能生出这种念头，她就觉得可怕。她宁可整个儿失去他，也不愿让自己良心上沾着这层暗示的污点。她把那杯酒放在他手边的小桌上，便想抽身退走。

“陪我待一会儿。”他说，“他们人都哪儿去了？”

“玛丽和你母亲在屋里。哈里和贝茨先生到那边看马去了。”

“我几乎觉得我也能走路了。”

===Original===

“You must not think of it yet, Mr. Medlicot. It seems almost a wonder that you shouldn’t have to be in bed, and you with your collarbone broken only last night! I don’t know how you can bear it as you do.”

“I shall be so glad I broke it, if one thing will come about.”

“What thing?” asked Kate, blushing.

“Kate﻿—may I call you Kate?”

“I don’t know,” she said.

===Chinese===

“现在还万万想不得，梅德利科特先生。您锁骨昨晚刚断，如今居然不必卧床，简直像是奇迹！我不知道您怎么撑得住。”

“只要有一件事能成，我断了它倒要谢天谢地。”

“什么事？”凯特红着脸问。

“凯特——我可以叫你凯特吗？”

“我不知道。”她说。

===Original===

“You know I love you﻿—do you not? You must know it. Dearest Kate, can you love me and be my wife?”

His left arm was bound up, and was in a sling, but he put out his right hand to take hers﻿—if she would give it to him. Kate Daly had never had a lover before, and felt the occasion to be trying. She had no doubt about the matter. If it were only proper for her to declare herself, she could swear with a safe conscience that she loved him better than all the world. “Put your hand here, Kate,” he said. As the request was not exactly for the gift of her hand, she placed it in his. “May I keep it now?” She could only whisper something which was quite inaudible, even to him. “I shall keep it, and think that you are all my own. Stoop down, Kate, and kiss me, if you love me.”

She hesitated for a moment, trying to collect her thoughts. She did love him, and was his own; still, to stoop and kiss a man who, if such a thing were to be allowed at all, ought certainly to kiss her! She did not think she could do that. But then she was bound to protect him, wounded and broken as he was, from his own imprudence; and if she did not stoop to him, he would rise to her. She was still in doubt, still standing with her hand in his, half bending over him, but yet half resisting as she bent, when, all suddenly, Harry Heathcote was on the veranda, followed by the two policemen, who had just returned from Boolabong. She was sure that Harry had seen her, and was by no means sure that she had been quick enough in escaping from her lover’s hand to have been unnoticed by the policemen also. She fled away as though guilty, and could hardly recover herself sufficiently to assist Mrs. Growler in producing the additional dinner which was required.

The two men were quickly sent to their rest, as has been told before; and Harry, who had in truth seen how close to his friend his sister-in-law had been standing, would, had it been possible, have restored the lovers to their old positions; but they were all now on the veranda, and it was impossible. Kate hung back, half in and half out of the sitting-room, and old Mrs. Medlicot had seated herself close to her son. Harry was lying at full length on a rug, and his wife was sitting over him. Then Giles Medlicot, who was not quite contented with the present condition of affairs, made a little speech.

“Mrs. Heathcote,” he said, “I have asked your sister to marry me.”

===Chinese===

“你知道我爱你——不是吗？你一定知道的。最亲爱的凯特，你能爱我，做我的妻子吗？”

他左臂缠着绷带，吊在悬带里，可他伸出右手来拉她的手——倘若她肯给的话。凯特·戴利从前不曾有过情人，只觉这场面难为情极了。这事她自己毫不含糊。倘若她表白得体，她尽可以问心无愧地起誓：她爱他胜过世上一切。“把手放在这儿，凯特。”他说。既然这一请求并非恰好是要“把手许给他”，她便把手放进了他的手里。“现在我可以留着它了吗？”她只能低低说了句什么，连他也听不清。“我要留着它，并且想，你整个人都是我的了。俯下身来，凯特，吻吻我，要是你爱我。”

她迟疑了片刻，想定一定神。她确实爱他，也确实是他的了；可是要她俯身去吻一个男人——倘若这种事竟被允许的话，也理应由他来吻她！她觉得自己做不来。可转念一想，他负了伤、断了骨头，她有责任保护他，不叫他由着性子乱来；她若不俯就他，他就要起身凑上来。她还在犹疑，还站在那里，手握在他的手里，半个身子朝他俯着，俯下时却又半推半就，正在这当口，哈里·希思科特猛然出现在游廊上，身后跟着刚从布拉邦回来的两名警察。她断定哈里看见了她的样子，却又拿不准自己从情人手里抽身抽得够不够快，有没有逃过警察的眼睛。她像做了亏心事似的逃开了，半天定不下神来帮格罗勒太太把添出来的那顿饭张罗上桌。

两名警察很快被打发去歇息，这是前面已经交代过的；哈里其实看清了小姨子同他的朋友贴得有多近，倘若可能，他真愿意让这对情人回到原来的姿势里去；可这会儿大家全在游廊上，办不到了。凯特退缩着，半在客厅里半在客厅外，梅德利科特老太太已经紧挨着儿子坐下。哈里直挺挺躺在一张毯子上，他妻子坐在他身旁看护。这时，吉尔斯·梅德利科特对眼下的局面不甚满意，便发了一小篇议论。

“希思科特太太，”他说，“我已经请求您的妹妹嫁给我了。”

===Original===

“Dearie me, Giles!” said Mrs. Medlicot.

Kate remained no longer half in and half out of the parlour, but retreated altogether and hid herself. Harry turned himself over on the rug, and looked up at his wife, claiming infinite credit in that he had foreseen that such a thing might happen.

“And what answer has she given you?” said Mrs. Heathcote.

“She hasn’t given me any answer yet. I wonder what you and Heathcote would say about it?”

===Chinese===

“哎呀我的天，吉尔斯！”梅德利科特太太说。

凯特再不是半进半出，索性整个儿退下去藏了起来。哈里在毯子上翻过身，仰望着妻子，一脸“我早料到会有这事”的居功之色。

“那她给了你什么答复？”希思科特太太问。

“她还没给我任何答复呢。不知道您和希思科特对这事怎么说？”

===Original===

“What Kate has to say is much more important,” replied the discreet sister.

“I should like it of all things,” said Harry, jumping up. “It’s always best to be open about these things. When you first came here, I didn’t like you. You took a bit of my river frontage﻿—not that it does me any great harm﻿—and then I was angry about that scoundrel Nokes.”

“I was wrong about Nokes,” said Medlicot, “and have, therefore, had my collarbone broken. As to the land, you’ll forgive my having it if Kate will come and live there?”

“By George! I should think so. Kate, why don’t you come out? Come along, my girl! Medlicot has spoken out openly, and you should answer him in the same fashion.”

===Chinese===

“凯特怎么说，那要紧得多。”这位有分寸的姐姐答道。

“我再乐意不过了。”哈里说着跳起身来。“这种事，敞开了说总是最好。你刚来的时候，我不喜欢你。你占了我一段河沿——倒也不是什么大损害——后来我又为诺克斯那个恶棍动气。”

“在诺克斯的事上是我错了，”梅德利科特说，“所以锁骨也断了。至于那块地，只要凯特肯去那儿一起住，您总肯原谅我占了它吧？”

“那还用说！我也这么想。凯特，你怎么不出来？出来吧，好姑娘！梅德利科特已经把话挑明了，你也该照样回他一句。”

===Original===

So saying, he dragged her forth, and I fear that, as far as she was concerned, something of the sweetness of her courtship was lost by the publicity with which she was forced to confess her love.

“Will you go, Kate, and make sugar down at the mill? I have often thought how bad it would be for Mary and me when you were taken away; but we shan’t mind it so much if we knew that you are to be near us.”

“Speak to him, Kate,” said Mrs. Heathcote, with her arm round her sister’s waist.

“I think she’s minded to have him,” said Mrs. Medlicot.

“Tell me, Kate﻿—shall it be so?” pleaded the lover.

===Chinese===

说着，他把她拽了出来。只怕就她而论，这场恋爱有一份甜蜜，就这么被当众逼出来的表白给毁了。

“你愿意去吗，凯特，到糖厂去熬糖？我常想，你嫁出去以后，玛丽和我会多难受；可只要知道你就在近旁，我们也就不至于那么难过了。”

“跟他说话呀，凯特。”希思科特太太搂着妹妹的腰说。

“我看她是打定主意要他了。”梅德利科特太太说。

“告诉我，凯特——就这么定了吗？”情人恳求道。

===Original===

She came up to him and leaned over him, and whispered one word which nobody else heard. But they all knew what the word was. And before they separated for the night she was left alone with him, and he got the kiss for which he was asking when the policemen interrupted them.

“That’s what I call a happy Christmas,” said Harry, as the party finally parted for the night.

===Chinese===

她走上前去，俯在他身上，低声说了一个字，谁也没有听见。可大家都知道那个字是什么。当夜众人散去之前，她又同他单独待了一会儿，他终于讨到了那个吻——警察们打岔时他正讨着的那个。

“这才叫快活的圣诞节。”哈里说，这时一伙人终于各自安歇去了。

## I Gangoil / I 冈戈伊尔

===Original===

Just a fortnight before Christmas, 1871, a young man, twenty-four years of age, returned home to his dinner about eight o’clock in the evening. He was married, and with him and his wife lived his wife’s sister. At that somewhat late hour he walked in among the two young women, and another much older woman who was preparing the table for dinner. The wife and the wife’s sister each had a child in her lap, the elder having seen some fifteen months of its existence, and the younger three months. “He has been out since seven, and I don’t think he’s had a mouthful,” the wife had just said. “Oh, Harry, you must be half starved,” she exclaimed, jumping up to greet him, and throwing her arm round his bare neck.

“I’m about whole melted,” he said, as he kissed her. “In the name of charity give me a nobbler. I did get a bit of damper and a pannikin of tea up at the German’s hut; but I never was so hot or so thirsty in my life. We’re going to have it in earnest this time. Old Bates says that when the gum leaves crackle, as they do now, before Christmas, there won’t be a blade of grass by the end of February.”

“I hate Old Bates,” said the wife. “He always prophesies evil, and complains about his rations.”

“He knows more about sheep than any man this side of the Mary,” said her husband.

From all this I trust the reader will understand that the Christmas to which he is introduced is not the Christmas with which he is intimate on this side of the equator﻿—a Christmas of blazing fires indoors, and of sleet amid snow and frost outside﻿—but the Christmas of Australia, in which happy land the Christmas fires are apt to be lighted﻿—or to light themselves﻿—when they are by no means needed.

===Chinese===

1871 年，离圣诞节整整还有两个星期的时候，一个二十四岁的年轻人，傍晚八点钟左右回到家里吃晚饭。他已成家，同他和妻子住在一起的，还有妻子的妹妹。在这个颇晚的时辰，他走进两位少妇中间，还有一位年长得多的老妇人，正在摆桌子备饭。妻子和小姨各抱着一个孩子，大的那个来到世上约莫十五个月，小的三个月。“他从七点就出去了，我看他一口东西也没吃过。”妻子刚才正这么说。“哦，哈里，你准是饿坏了。”她喊着跳起身来迎他，一把搂住他赤裸的脖子。

“我简直要整个儿化掉了。”他吻着她说道。“发发慈悲，给我弄一小杯烧酒吧。在德国人的小屋里我倒是吃过一块硬面饼、喝过一缸子茶；可我这辈子从没这么热、这么渴过。这一回可是要动真格的了。老贝茨说，桉树叶像现在这样、还没到圣诞节就噼啪作响，到二月底就一根草也剩不下了。”

“我讨厌老贝茨。”妻子说，“他总是预言灾祸，还老抱怨口粮。”

“在玛丽河这一岸，论懂羊，谁也比不上他。”她丈夫说。

我相信，读者从这些话里已经明白，此刻给他引见的这个圣诞节，不是他在赤道这一侧所熟悉的那个圣诞节——屋内炉火熊熊、屋外雨雪交加霜冻彻骨的圣诞节——而是澳大利亚的圣诞节。在那片幸运的国土上，圣诞之火往往在最不需要的时候被人点燃——或者自己烧起来。

===Original===

The young man who had just returned home had on a flannel shirt, a pair of moleskin trousers, and an old straw hat, battered nearly out of all shape. He had no coat, no waistcoat, no braces, and nothing round his neck. Round his waist there was a strap or belt, from the front of which hung a small pouch, and, behind, a knife in a case. And stuck into a loop in the belt, made for the purpose, there was a small brierwood pipe. As he dashed his hat off, wiped his brow, and threw himself into a rocking-chair, he certainly was rough to look at, but by all who understood Australian life he would have been taken to be a gentleman. He was a young squatter, well known west of the Mary River, in Queensland. Harry Heathcote of Gangoil, who owned 30,000 sheep of his own, was a magistrate in those parts, and able to hold his own among his neighbours, whether rough or gentle; and some neighbours he had, very rough, who made it almost necessary that a man should be able to be rough also, on occasions, if he desired to live among them without injury. Heathcote of Gangoil could do all that. Men said of him that he was too imperious, too masterful, too much inclined to think that all things should be made to go as he would have them. Young as he was, he had been altogether his own master since he was of age﻿—and not only his own master, but the master also of all with whom he was brought into contact from day to day. In his life he conversed but seldom with any but those who were dependent on him, nor had he done so for the last three years. At an age at which young men at home are still subject to pastors and masters, he had sprung at once into patriarchal power, and, being a man determined to thrive, had become laborious and thoughtful beyond his years.

Harry Heathcote had been left an orphan, with a small fortune in money, when he was fourteen. For two years after that he had consented to remain quietly at school, but at sixteen he declared his purpose of emigrating. Boys less than himself in stature got above him at school, and he had not liked it. For a twelvemonth he was opposed by his guardian; but at the end of the year he was fitted forth for the colony. The guardian was not sorry to be quit of him, but prophesied that he would be home again before a year was over. The lad had not returned, and it was now a settled conviction among all who knew him that he would make or mar his fortune in the new land that he had chosen.

He was a tall, well-made young fellow, with fair hair and a good-humoured smile, but ever carrying in his countenance marks of what his enemies called pigheadedness, his acquaintances obstinacy, and those who loved him firmness. His acquaintances were, perhaps, right, for he certainly was obstinate. He would take no man’s advice, he would submit himself to no man, and in the conduct of his own business preferred to trust to his own insight than to the experience of others. It would sometimes occur that he had to pay heavily for his obstinacy. But, on the other hand, the lessons which he learned he learned thoroughly. And he was kept right in his trade by his own indefatigable industry. That trade was the growth of wool. He was a breeder of sheep on a Queensland sheep-run, and his flocks ran far afield over a vast territory of which he was the only lord. His house was near the river Mary, and beyond the river his domain did not extend; but around him on his own side of the river he could ride for ten miles in each direction without getting off his own pastures. He was master, as far as his mastership went, of 120,000 acres﻿—almost an English county﻿—and it was the pride of his heart to put his foot off his own territory as seldom as possible. He sent his wool annually down to Brisbane, and received his stores, tea and sugar, flour and brandy, boots, clothes, tobacco, etc., once or twice a year from thence. But the traffic did not require his own presence at the city. So self-contained was the working of the establishment that he was never called away by his business, unless he went to see some lot of highly bred sheep which he might feel disposed to buy; and as for pleasure, it had come to be altogether beyond the purpose of his life to go in quest of that. When the work of the day was over, he would lie at his length upon rugs in the veranda, with a pipe in his mouth, while his wife sat over him reading a play of Shakespeare or the last novel that had come to them from England.

He had married a fair girl, the orphan daughter of a bankrupt squatter whom he had met in Sydney, and had brought her and her sister into the Queensland bush with him. His wife idolized him. His sister-in-law, Kate Daly, loved him dearly﻿—as she had cause to do, for he had proved himself to be a very brother to her; but she feared him also somewhat. The people about the Mary said that she was fairer and sweeter to look at even than the elder sister. Mrs. Heathcote was the taller of the two, and the larger-featured. She certainly was the higher in intellect, and the fittest to be the mistress of such an establishment as that at Gangoil.

When he had washed his hands and face, and had swallowed the very copious but weak allowance of brandy-and-water which his wife mixed for him, he took the eldest boy on his lap and fondled him.

===Chinese===

这个刚回家的年轻人身穿法兰绒衬衫、一条斜纹绒布裤，头上一顶旧草帽，磕碰得几乎走了形。他没有外衣，没有背心，没有吊带，脖子上什么也没有。腰间扎着一根皮带，前面坠着一只小袋，后面别着一把带鞘的刀。皮带上还专设了一个小环，里面插着一支石南根小烟斗。他一把抓掉帽子，抹了抹额头，一屁股坐进摇椅，模样看上去确实粗野，可在一切懂得澳大利亚生活的人眼里，他都会被认作绅士。他是玛丽河以西、昆士兰境内一位小有名气的年轻牧场主。冈戈伊尔的哈里·希思科特（Harry Heathcote of Gangoil），拥有自家 30,000 只羊，是那一带的治安官，无论左邻右舍粗野还是斯文，他都能立得住脚；他的邻居中确有一些非常粗野的，弄得一个人倘若想不受伤害地住在他们中间，几乎必须也会在必要时来一手粗的。冈戈伊尔的希思科特这些样样来得。人们说他太专横、太霸道，太容易认为万事都该照他的意思办。他虽然年轻，自打成年起就完完全全自己做主了——不但自己做主，还做一切与他日日打交道之人的主。平素他极少同不仰他鼻息的人交谈，三年来一直如此。在本土的年轻人还得听命于牧师和师长的年纪，他已一跃而执家长之权；他又是个立志发达的人，于是勤勉多思，远超他的年龄。

哈里·希思科特十四岁上成了孤儿，名下留有一小笔钱财。此后有两年，他勉强同意安安静静待在学校里，可一到十六岁，他便宣布要移居海外。学校里个子比他小的男孩都爬到了他上头，他不喜欢这个。整整一年，监护人跟他拗着；可到了年底，他被置办齐整，送往殖民地。监护人摆脱了他并不觉得遗憾，只是预言不出一年他准会回家。这小伙子没有回来，如今凡认识他的人都已认准：他会在自己选中的这片新土地上或成或败。

他是个身材高挑、体格匀称的年轻人，一头金发，脸上常挂着和气的微笑，但眉宇间永远带着某种印记：他的仇人称之为死心眼，他的相识称之为固执，爱他的人称之为坚定。大概他的相识说得对，因为他确实固执。他不听任何人的劝，不服任何人的管，经营自己的产业，宁可信赖自己的眼光，也不信赖旁人的经验。有时他难免为固执付出高昂的代价。但另一方面，他学到的功课都学得透彻。而凭着他自己不知疲倦的勤勉，他这一行从来没有走偏。他这一行就是产羊毛。他是昆士兰一个牧羊场上的养羊人，他的羊群散布在广袤的领地上，那片土地上他是唯一的君主。他的宅子临近玛丽河，河那一边他的领地便不再延伸；可在河的这一边，他朝任何一个方向骑上十英里都出不了自家的牧场。他名下，在他权力所及的范围内，有 120,000 英亩——几乎抵得上英国的一个郡——能尽量不把脚踏出自家地界，正是他心中的一件得意事。他每年把羊毛运往布里斯班，茶叶、糖、面粉、白兰地、靴子、衣裳、烟叶等一应用品，每年从那里收一两回。可这类往来并不需要他亲赴城市。这片产业的运转如此自给自足，生意从不会把他召走，除非去看某批他有意买下的良种羊；至于寻欢作乐，那早已全然不在他人生的目的之内。一天的事做完，他便直挺挺躺在游廊的毯子上，嘴里叼着烟斗，他妻子坐在一旁，给他读莎士比亚的戏文，或是刚从英国寄到的最新小说。

他娶了一位美丽的姑娘，是他在悉尼结识的一个破了产的牧场主的孤女，并把姑娘和她妹妹一起带进了昆士兰的丛林。妻子把他奉若神明。小姨子凯特·戴利也真心爱他——她有理由如此，因为他待她实在亲如兄长；可她也有些怕他。玛丽河一带的人说，论容貌的秀丽甜美，她比姐姐还要胜过几分。希思科特太太是姐妹中较高的一个，五官也较大。她的智力无疑更高，也最配做冈戈伊尔这样一户人家的主母。

他洗净了手脸，灌下妻子为他调的那一大杯掺水的淡白兰地，然后把大儿子抱到膝上逗弄。

===Original===

“By George!” he said, “old fellow, you shan’t be a squatter.”

“Why not, Harry?” asked his wife.

“Because I don’t want him to break his heart every day of his life.”

“Are you always breaking yours? I thought your heart was pretty well hardened now.”

“When a man talks of his heart, you and Kate are thinking of loves and doves, of course.”

===Chinese===

“天哪！”他说，“小家伙，你可不许当牧场主。”

“为什么不许，哈里？”妻子问。

“因为我不想让他一辈子天天伤心。”

“你的心是天天在伤吗？我还以为你的心早就磨出茧子了。”

“一个男人说起他的心，你和凯特想的当然是情啊爱啊那一套。”
